Theorem 1 (Certified gain retention). Let � be any feasible fractional coloring with total weight $\Lambda = \Lambda ( \lambda )$ ). Under Assumption 1, for every realization ofthe agent proposals,

$$

$$

In particular, an optimal fractional coloring gives $\Lambda = \chi _ { f } ( G )$ and therefore retains at least a $1 / \chi _ { f } ( G )$ fraction of the total verified singleton gain.

Theorem 2 (Tightness of certified gain retention). For any finite nonempty dependency graph �, no coordinator thatforms its update by selecting a subset ofthe submitted proposals can guarantee retaining more than a $1 / \chi _ { f } ( G )$ fraction of the total verified singleton gain in the worst case. More precisely, for every such $G ,$ there exists an instance satisfying Assumption 1 in which the submitted proposals have total verified gain $\chi _ { f } ( G )$ , while every subset ofthem achieves gain at most one. The bound remains tight when all singleton gains are required to be strictly positive, in which case the worst-case ratio can be made arbitrarily close to $1 / \chi _ { f } ( G )$

## A Repair benchmark and evaluation protocols

Table 6: Tasks in the three-module repair benchmark. Each task has a path dependency graph. “Lines” counts faulty editable code; “Checks” gives visible/hidden check counts; and $F ( x ^ { 0 } )$ is the initial number of failures. “Double” counts the integration failures caused by applying the interface transformation in both adjacent modules when all other code is correct.

Repair schedules and stopping rules. All protocols stop when � = 0 or when they reach their prescribed limit. SGL allows up to four whole-program repair sessions, each starting from the most recently accepted program. Merge decisions. The parallel protocols difer in how they combine those proposals. MA applies their union directly, while MAG accepts the union only when it reduces � . COL evaluates each changed module separately against the starting program, retains strictly improving proposals, and selects the color class with the largest summed gain, as in (6). OPT also evaluates the union of the retained proposals and selects it when its <sub>g</sub>ain exceeds that of the color candidate (13).

Reusing deferred proposals. First merges and the interaction surplus. When ownership is consistent, repairs in adjacent modules can reinforce each other by making their shared inte<sub>g</sub>ration checks <sub>p</sub>ass. The first union solved all 12 conflict-free claude-haiku-4-5 units and all 5 conflict-free claude-sonnet-5 units. Merge-all and OPT therefore finished in one round, while the color candidate left a mean of 12.3 and 11.6 failing checks, respectively. COL needed a further round. On the 9 claude-haiku-4-5 units with conflicts, the first mer<sub>g</sub>e left a mean of 5.3 failures for mer<sub>g</sub>e-all, 9.1 for COL, and 5.1 for OPT.

Across both experiments, OPT evaluated 37 unions, accepted 26, and selected the color candidate 11 times. Outcomes and first merges. Every protocol passed all visible and hidden checks on every instance it ran. SGL completed each task in one session, and SEQ completed each in one pass. MA, MAG, and OPT usually finished in one round. Their exception was the third 16-module instance (S16c in Table 12), with trajector<sub>y</sub> 182, 2, 0: the invoice a<sub>g</sub>ent left one function unre<sub>p</sub>aired, and the same two checks failed when its proposal was tested alone against the reference. COL completed repair after re-verifying its deferred class, as in the trajectory 386, 153, 0.

Figure 8: Fraction of initially failing checks repaired over time for tasks with (a) 16 and (b) 32 modules. Single-agent curves evaluate workspace snapshots taken every five seconds on visible and hidden checks. Team curves model simultaneous firstround starts and immediate merging as each call finishes, with no verification delay. Each curve represents one instance; the team curves provide an optimistic merge-on-finish reference for comparison with the single-agent runs.

## H Exploratory fractional route-search schedule

Scheduling question. When local-search agents share vehicle routes, exclusive route access limits which searches can run concurrently. We compare the duration of ordinary and fractional preemptible execution schedules under these conflicts. ## J.1 Proof of Theorem 5

Theorem 5 combines three ine<sub>q</sub>ualities: the mer<sub>g</sub>e retains local <sub>g</sub>ains<sub>,</sub> conditional a<sub>g</sub>ent-<sub>q</sub>ualit<sub>y</sub> bounds relate those <sub>g</sub>ains to exact block i<sub>mprovemen</sub>t<sub>, an</sub>d th<sub>e</sub> bl<sub>oc</sub>k <sub>error</sub> b<sub>oun</sub>d <sub>re</sub>l<sub>a</sub>t<sub>es</sub> th<sub>a</sub>t i<sub>mprovemen</sub>t t<sub>o g</sub>l<sub>o</sub>b<sub>a</sub>l <sub>error.</sub>

Proof. Expected local progress. Write $\mathbb { E } _ { k } [ \cdot ] : = \mathbb { E } [ \cdot \mid \mathcal { H } _ { k } ]$ <sub>an</sub>d $\mathbb { E } _ { k , M } [ \cdot ] : = \mathbb { E } [ \cdot \mid \mathcal { H } _ { k } , M ]$ <sub>.</sub> C<sub>on</sub>diti<sub>on on</sub> th<sub>e</sub> hi<sub>s</sub>t<sub>ory</sub> b<sub>e</sub>f<sub>ore</sub> d<sub>raw</sub>i<sub>ng</sub> th<sub>e</sub> matchin<sub>g</sub>. A<sub>pp</sub>l<sub>y</sub> Pro<sub>p</sub>osition 4 (or Theorem 1 for the safe-onl<sub>y</sub> method), then condition on each matchin<sub>g</sub> and use Assum<sub>p</sub>tion 2:

$$
\begin{array} { r l } { \mathbb { E } _ { k } \big [ F ( x ^ { k } ) - F ( x ^ { k + 1 } ) \big ] } & { \stackrel { ( 1 4 ) } { \geq } \displaystyle \frac { 1 } { \Lambda } \sum _ { M } { p _ { M } \sum _ { ( i , b ) \in { \mathcal M } } \mathbb { E } _ { k , M } [ g _ { i b } ^ { k } ] } } \\ & { \stackrel { ( 1 5 ) } { \geq } \displaystyle \frac { 1 } { \Lambda } \sum _ { M } \hat { p } _ { M } \displaystyle \sum _ { ( i , b ) \in { \mathcal M } } \eta _ { i b } ( h ) D _ { b } ( x ^ { k } ) } \\ & { \stackrel { ( 1 9 ) } { = } \displaystyle \frac { 1 } { \Lambda } \sum _ { b } a _ { b } D _ { b } ( x ^ { k } ) \geq \displaystyle \frac { a } { \Lambda } \sum _ { b } D _ { b } ( x ^ { k } ) \stackrel { ( 1 6 ) ( 1 9 ) } { \geq } \theta _ { \lambda } \Delta ( x ^ { k } ) . } \end{array}\tag{31}
$$

The <sub>p</sub>enultimate ine<sub>q</sub>ualit<sub>y</sub> uses $a _ { b } \geq a$ from (19) and $D _ { b } ( x ^ { k } ) \geq 0 ,$ which follows because leavin<sub>g</sub> the block unchan<sub>g</sub>ed is feasible in (1). From progress to error. Subtract the improvement from the old objective gap:

$$
\begin{array} { r l } & { \mathbb { E } _ { k } [ \Delta ( x ^ { k + 1 } ) ] \stackrel { ( 1 ) } { = } \Delta ( x ^ { k } ) - \mathbb { E } _ { k } [ F ( x ^ { k } ) - F ( x ^ { k + 1 } ) ] } \\ & { \qquad \stackrel { ( 3 1 ) } { \leq } ( 1 - \theta _ { \lambda } ) \Delta ( x ^ { k } ) . } \end{array}\tag{32}
$$

Write $\boldsymbol { e } _ { k } : = \mathbb { E } \left[ \Delta ( \boldsymbol { x } ^ { k } ) \right]$ . Takin<sub>g</sub> ex<sub>p</sub>ectations in the conditional bound <sub>g</sub>ives

$$
\begin{array} { r } { \stackrel { ( 3 2 ) } { \leq } ( 1 - \theta _ { \lambda } ) e _ { k } . } \end{array}\tag{33}
$$

Th<sub>e</sub> b<sub>ase case</sub> i<sub>s</sub> $e _ { 0 } = \Delta ( x ^ { 0 } ) . \mathrm { I f } e _ { k } \le ( 1 - \theta _ { \lambda } ) ^ { k } e _ { 0 } ,$ <sub>,</sub> th<sub>en</sub>

$$
\begin{array} { r } { e _ { k + 1 } \stackrel { ( 3 3 ) } { \leq } ( 1 - \theta _ { \lambda } ) e _ { k } \leq ( 1 - \theta _ { \lambda } ) ^ { k + 1 } e _ { 0 } , } \end{array}\tag{34}
$$

where the second ine<sub>q</sub>ualit<sub>y</sub> is the induction h<sub>yp</sub>othesis and uses $1 - \theta _ { \lambda } \ge 0$ . This <sub>p</sub>roves (21). If $\dot { \theta } _ { \lambda } = 0$ <sub>,</sub> the concl<sub>u</sub>sion <sub>g</sub>i<sub>v</sub>es monotonicit<sub>y</sub> on<sup>l</sup><sub>y</sub>. $\operatorname { I f } \theta _ { \lambda } = 1$ <sub>,</sub> nonne<sub>g</sub>ativit<sub>y</sub> forces a zero <sub>g</sub>a<sub>p</sub> after one round<sub>;</sub> the case $K = 0$ means the unchan<sub>g</sub>ed initial <sub>g</sub>a<sub>p</sub>. □

## K Extended related work

Graph-theoretic context. Broader work on graph coloring and related extremal questions includes [11–13, 22, 44].

Agent systems. Further work on agent interaction and orchestration includes [9, 10, 17, 51]; empirical studies of collaboration include [7, 27, 38]. Ilin and Richtárik [25] anal<sub>y</sub>ze conver<sub>g</sub>ence of corrected local search with verified mer<sub>g</sub>in<sub>g</sub>.

Distributed optimization and training. Work on time, allocation, and communication includes [19, 33, 34, 50]. Related work on distributed model trainin<sub>g</sub> includes [14, 15, 20, 28–30, 35, 37, 42, 43]; other o<sub>p</sub>timization and model-ada<sub>p</sub>tation methods include [24, 26, 47].
